========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess I’m here because it’s gotten a bit ridiculous with wasps. I mean, I used to be out in the yard all summer, and I helped with the garden jobs at church — the flowers and weeding and all that, you know, they’ve got those beds by the entrance — but now I plan almost my whole summer around avoiding them.

If I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out there. I’ll make my husband unload things instead, even if it’s just groceries or something. It’s embarrassing, really, but I’ve started giving things up because of it.

Yes, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it a full panic attack, exactly, but it feels very sudden. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands start shaking.

I just have to get back in the house right away.

Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.

My sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.

It sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.

Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.

It’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.

I’ll make my husband unload things instead, even if it’s just groceries or something. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”

I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.

I mean, I used to be out in the yard all summer, and I helped with the garden jobs at church — the flowers and weeding and all that, you know, they’ve got those beds by the entrance — but now I plan almost my whole summer around avoiding them.\n\nIf I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out there. I’ll make my husband unload things instead, even if it’s just groceries or something. It’s embarrassing, really, but I’ve started giving things up because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or a panic attack when you see a wasp or think you might see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it a full panic attack, exactly, but it feels very sudden. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands start shaking.\n\nAnd then all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.” It’s silly because sometimes it’s just near the flowers and not even coming toward me — and my husband always says, “Just leave it alone and it’ll leave you alone,” which, maybe that’s true, but in the moment I can’t think like that. I just have to get back in the house right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations because you're afraid of others seeing you react to wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because of people seeing me, exactly. I mean, I don’t like making a scene, nobody does, and at church there are always people coming in and out by those flower beds, and you know how folks notice everything… but I think mostly I’m just trying not to be near the wasps.\n\nI suppose if someone saw me jump or hurry inside, I’d be embarrassed, yes. But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.

But really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.

But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mainly about the wasp itself or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that resembles a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s not just the wasp itself, I don’t think. It’s that if it looks like one, then my mind goes straight to being stung. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and keep talking—my neighbor does that outside all the time—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. \n\nSo, yes, it’s the possibility of being stung too, especially if the insect resembles a wasp.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Maybe a little less, if I truly knew it wasn’t a wasp. But honestly, in the moment, I don’t think I get that far. I see something small and striped or hear it buzzing, and my body reacts before my head catches up.

It’s embarrassing, really, but I’ve started giving things up because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or a panic attack when you see a wasp or think you might see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it a full panic attack, exactly, but it feels very sudden. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands start shaking.\n\nAnd then all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.” It’s silly because sometimes it’s just near the flowers and not even coming toward me — and my husband always says, “Just leave it alone and it’ll leave you alone,” which, maybe that’s true, but in the moment I can’t think like that. I just have to get back in the house right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations because you're afraid of others seeing you react to wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because of people seeing me, exactly. I mean, I don’t like making a scene, nobody does, and at church there are always people coming in and out by those flower beds, and you know how folks notice everything… but I think mostly I’m just trying not to be near the wasps.\n\nI suppose if someone saw me jump or hurry inside, I’d be embarrassed, yes. But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mainly about the wasp itself or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that resembles a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s not just the wasp itself, I don’t think. It’s that if it looks like one, then my mind goes straight to being stung. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and keep talking—my neighbor does that outside all the time—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. \n\nSo, yes, it’s the possibility of being stung too, especially if the insect resembles a wasp.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be significantly less if you knew for certain that the insect you saw was not a wasp, but something similar-looking?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less, if I truly knew it wasn’t a wasp. But honestly, in the moment, I don’t think I get that far. I see something small and striped or hear it buzzing, and my body reacts before my head catches up.\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that at cookouts, like it’s nothing at all—but to me, if one gets near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So even if someone told me, “No, that’s not a wasp,” I might still be scared until it was away from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Comfortable? Not really, no. I mean, if they’re way off somewhere and I’m inside behind a window, maybe I can stand it, but if they’re actually around me… no.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because it’s gotten a bit ridiculous with wasps. I mean, I used to be out in the yard all summer, and I helped with the garden jobs at church — the flowers and weeding and all that, you know, they’ve got those beds by the entrance — but now I plan almost my whole summer around avoiding them.\n\nIf I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out there. I’ll make my husband unload things instead, even if it’s just groceries or something. It’s embarrassing, really, but I’ve started giving things up because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or a panic attack when you see a wasp or think you might see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it a full panic attack, exactly, but it feels very sudden. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands start shaking.\n\nAnd then all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.” It’s silly because sometimes it’s just near the flowers and not even coming toward me — and my husband always says, “Just leave it alone and it’ll leave you alone,” which, maybe that’s true, but in the moment I can’t think like that. I just have to get back in the house right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations because you're afraid of others seeing you react to wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because of people seeing me, exactly. I mean, I don’t like making a scene, nobody does, and at church there are always people coming in and out by those flower beds, and you know how folks notice everything… but I think mostly I’m just trying not to be near the wasps.\n\nI suppose if someone saw me jump or hurry inside, I’d be embarrassed, yes. But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mainly about the wasp itself or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that resembles a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s not just the wasp itself, I don’t think. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and keep talking—my neighbor does that outside all the time—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. \n\nSo, yes, it’s the possibility of being stung too, especially if the insect resembles a wasp.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be significantly less if you knew for certain that the insect you saw was not a wasp, but something similar-looking?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less, if I truly knew it wasn’t a wasp. But honestly, in the moment, I don’t think I get that far. I see something small and striped or hear it buzzing, and my body reacts before my head catches up.\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that at cookouts, like it’s nothing at all—but to me, if one gets near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So even if someone told me, “No, that’s not a wasp,” I might still be scared until it was away from me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you would feel comfortable around wasps, even if they are present?\n\nPatient: Comfortable? And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me going out there.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because other people just deal with it, but I just don’t feel comfortable if they’re present.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mainly about the wasp itself or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that resembles a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s not just the wasp itself, I don’t think. It’s that if it looks like one, then my mind goes straight to being stung. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and keep talking—my neighbor does that outside all the time—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. \n\nSo, yes, it’s the possibility of being stung too, especially if the insect resembles a wasp.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be significantly less if you knew for certain that the insect you saw was not a wasp, but something similar-looking?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less, if I truly knew it wasn’t a wasp. But honestly, in the moment, I don’t think I get that far. I see something small and striped or hear it buzzing, and my body reacts before my head catches up.\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that at cookouts, like it’s nothing at all—but to me, if one gets near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So even if someone told me, “No, that’s not a wasp,” I might still be scared until it was away from me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you would feel comfortable around wasps, even if they are present?\n\nPatient: Comfortable? Not really, no. I mean, if they’re way off somewhere and I’m inside behind a window, maybe I can stand it, but if they’re actually around me… no.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I’ve gotten so I plan my whole summer around avoiding them. I used to help with the garden work at church, you know, pulling weeds and planting things, and they always need people for that, but I’ve stopped doing it because I’m afraid there’ll be wasps around. And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me going out there.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because other people just deal with it, but I just don’t feel comfortable if they’re present.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps specifically about wasps themselves, or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that looks similar to a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s more the wasp, I think… well, no, it’s the whole idea of being stung if it looks like one. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice—my sister does that, she’ll be sitting there with her iced tea like nothing’s happening—but to me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So if another insect looked enough like a wasp, I’d probably react the same way at first.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I think it’s both, in a way. I know a wasp is just a little insect—people swat them away all the time like it’s nothing. My neighbor does that when we’re sitting outside, and she’ll just keep talking about her flowers.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because it’s gotten a bit ridiculous with wasps. I mean, I used to be out in the yard all summer, and I helped with the garden jobs at church — the flowers and weeding and all that, you know, they’ve got those beds by the entrance — but now I plan almost my whole summer around avoiding them.\n\nIf I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out there. I’ll make my husband unload things instead, even if it’s just groceries or something. It’s embarrassing, really, but I’ve started giving things up because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or a panic attack when you see a wasp or think you might see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it a full panic attack, exactly, but it feels very sudden. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands start shaking.\n\nAnd then all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.” It’s silly because sometimes it’s just near the flowers and not even coming toward me — and my husband always says, “Just leave it alone and it’ll leave you alone,” which, maybe that’s true, but in the moment I can’t think like that. I just have to get back in the house right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations because you're afraid of others seeing you react to wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because of people seeing me, exactly. I mean, I don’t like making a scene, nobody does, and at church there are always people coming in and out by those flower beds, and you know how folks notice everything… but I think mostly I’m just trying not to be near the wasps.\n\nI suppose if someone saw me jump or hurry inside, I’d be embarrassed, yes. But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mainly about the wasp itself or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that resembles a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s not just the wasp itself, I don’t think. It’s that if it looks like one, then my mind goes straight to being stung. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and keep talking—my neighbor does that outside all the time—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. \n\nSo, yes, it’s the possibility of being stung too, especially if the insect resembles a wasp.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be significantly less if you knew for certain that the insect you saw was not a wasp, but something similar-looking?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less, if I truly knew it wasn’t a wasp. But honestly, in the moment, I don’t think I get that far. I see something small and striped or hear it buzzing, and my body reacts before my head catches up.\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that at cookouts, like it’s nothing at all—but to me, if one gets near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So even if someone told me, “No, that’s not a wasp,” I might still be scared until it was away from me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you would feel comfortable around wasps, even if they are present?\n\nPatient: Comfortable? Not really, no. I mean, if they’re way off somewhere and I’m inside behind a window, maybe I can stand it, but if they’re actually around me… no.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I’ve gotten so I plan my whole summer around avoiding them. I used to help with the garden work at church, you know, pulling weeds and planting things, and they always need people for that, but I’ve stopped doing it because I’m afraid there’ll be wasps around. And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me going out there.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because other people just deal with it, but I just don’t feel comfortable if they’re present.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps specifically about wasps themselves, or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that looks similar to a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s more the wasp, I think… well, no, it’s the whole idea of being stung if it looks like one. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice—my sister does that, she’ll be sitting there with her iced tea like nothing’s happening—but to me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So if another insect looked enough like a wasp, I’d probably react the same way at first.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps solely about the wasp itself or also about the pain or consequences of being stung?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, in a way. I know a wasp is just a little insect—people swat them away all the time like it’s nothing. My neighbor does that when we’re sitting outside, and she’ll just keep talking about her flowers.\n\nBut for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it doesn’t feel little. It feels like something terrible is about to happen. The sting, yes, that’s part of it, but it’s almost like my body reacts to the wasp itself before I can even think it through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me going out there.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because other people just deal with it, but I just don’t feel comfortable if they’re present.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps specifically about wasps themselves, or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that looks similar to a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s more the wasp, I think… well, no, it’s the whole idea of being stung if it looks like one. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice—my sister does that, she’ll be sitting there with her iced tea like nothing’s happening—but to me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So if another insect looked enough like a wasp, I’d probably react the same way at first.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps solely about the wasp itself or also about the pain or consequences of being stung?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, in a way. I know a wasp is just a little insect—people swat them away all the time like it’s nothing. My neighbor does that when we’re sitting outside, and she’ll just keep talking about her flowers.\n\nBut for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it doesn’t feel little. It feels like something terrible is about to happen. The sting, yes, that’s part of it, but it’s almost like my body reacts to the wasp itself before I can even think it through.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any specific situations or contexts where your fear of wasps is less intense?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know a wasp is just a little insect and most folks would swat it away, but to me it feels like something terrible is about to happen if one gets anywhere near me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe if it’s far enough away that I can tell it’s not coming toward me, it’s a little less… I don’t know, sharp. Like if I see one out in the yard from inside the house, I can keep myself together better.

But if it gets anywhere near me, that’s when it changes. I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would just swat it away and go on talking—my husband does that, and then he acts like I’m overreacting—but to me it feels like something awful is about to happen. Even if nothing has happened yet, just it being close makes me feel like I need to get away.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because it’s gotten a bit ridiculous with wasps. I mean, I used to be out in the yard all summer, and I helped with the garden jobs at church — the flowers and weeding and all that, you know, they’ve got those beds by the entrance — but now I plan almost my whole summer around avoiding them.\n\nIf I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out there. I’ll make my husband unload things instead, even if it’s just groceries or something. It’s embarrassing, really, but I’ve started giving things up because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or a panic attack when you see a wasp or think you might see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it a full panic attack, exactly, but it feels very sudden. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands start shaking.\n\nAnd then all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.” It’s silly because sometimes it’s just near the flowers and not even coming toward me — and my husband always says, “Just leave it alone and it’ll leave you alone,” which, maybe that’s true, but in the moment I can’t think like that. I just have to get back in the house right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations because you're afraid of others seeing you react to wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s because of people seeing me, exactly. I mean, I don’t like making a scene, nobody does, and at church there are always people coming in and out by those flower beds, and you know how folks notice everything… but I think mostly I’m just trying not to be near the wasps.\n\nI suppose if someone saw me jump or hurry inside, I’d be embarrassed, yes. But I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh no, everyone will see me.” It’s more that I don’t want to be out there if there might be one.\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of bees or other insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Bees… well, I don’t love them either, I suppose, but wasps are the ones that really set me off. I know a wasp is just a little insect — I mean, most people would probably just wave it away or swat at it and get on with things — but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen.\n\nIt’s funny, my neighbor has all these flowers along her fence, and she’ll stand right there watering them like it’s nothing, and I’ll be on my side thinking, “How are you so calm?” But yes, it’s mainly wasps. They just make me feel like I need to get away immediately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations where you feel safe from wasps, or is it more about the wasps themselves that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: I suppose I feel safer inside, or if I know there aren’t flowers or trash cans nearby, but honestly it’s more the wasp itself. Like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away and not think twice.\n\nMy sister does that, actually—she’ll just wave her hand like she’s brushing away a bit of dust, and I’m standing there thinking, “Don’t do that, you’ll make it mad.” But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s not really the place as much as knowing there might be a wasp there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be just as strong if you were in a situation where you couldn't see the wasp but knew it was nearby, like in a closed room?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, if I knew there was one in the room, even if I couldn’t see it, I’d be on edge. I’d probably be staring around trying to figure out where it went.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because I know a wasp is just a little insect, and plenty of people would just swat it or shoo it away. My brother-in-law does that all the time at cookouts, like it’s nothing, while I’m thinking, “Please don’t make it come over here.” But to me, if one is anywhere near me, even hidden somewhere, it feels like something terrible could happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear other flying insects, like hornets or yellow jackets, as much as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: Hornets and yellow jackets… I guess I sort of put them in the same category, you know, anything that looks like it could sting me and comes buzzing around. But wasps are the main one I think about.\n\nIt’s like, I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that, even while she’s holding a plate at a picnic, which I cannot understand—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So yes, those others can scare me too, but wasps are the ones that really get me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear hornets or yellow jackets to the same degree as you fear wasps?\n\nPatient: I think they scare me, yes, if they’re buzzing around me, but wasps are the one I get stuck on in my head. Hornets and yellow jackets kind of blur together with them for me, like, “that thing can sting me.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and go on with their day—my neighbor does that when she’s gardening, she’ll just flick her hand and keep talking about her tomatoes—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So maybe not exactly the same degree, but close, because they remind me of wasps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps also applies to other flying insects that are not wasps, but might resemble them in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, if they look enough like a wasp or they’re buzzing near me. I don’t always stop and identify exactly what kind it is—I just see that shape or hear that sound and my mind jumps to, “Oh no.”\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would swat it away and not think twice. My sister is like that—she’ll be sitting outside with her iced tea, just waving at bugs like she’s shooing dust. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, or even something that looks like it might be one, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: Is it something specific about the way wasps look or behave that scares you, or is it more about the idea of being stung?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s both, but mostly the being stung. The way they look and move sets me off, that darting around, like you can’t predict where they’re going. And that buzzing—well, my mother used to say you could tell the difference between bees and wasps by how they fly, though I never really could, and she was always calmer about those things than I am.\n\nBut really, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away and keep doing whatever they’re doing. To me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. Like my mind goes straight to getting stung before I can even think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mainly about the wasp itself or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that resembles a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s not just the wasp itself, I don’t think. It’s that if it looks like one, then my mind goes straight to being stung. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably just swat it away and keep talking—my neighbor does that outside all the time—but for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. \n\nSo, yes, it’s the possibility of being stung too, especially if the insect resembles a wasp.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be significantly less if you knew for certain that the insect you saw was not a wasp, but something similar-looking?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less, if I truly knew it wasn’t a wasp. But honestly, in the moment, I don’t think I get that far. I see something small and striped or hear it buzzing, and my body reacts before my head catches up.\n\nI know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away and go on talking—my cousin does that at cookouts, like it’s nothing at all—but to me, if one gets near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So even if someone told me, “No, that’s not a wasp,” I might still be scared until it was away from me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you would feel comfortable around wasps, even if they are present?\n\nPatient: Comfortable? Not really, no. I mean, if they’re way off somewhere and I’m inside behind a window, maybe I can stand it, but if they’re actually around me… no.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me going out there.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because other people just deal with it, but I just don’t feel comfortable if they’re present.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps specifically about wasps themselves, or is it also about the possibility of being stung by any insect that looks similar to a wasp?\n\nPatient: It’s more the wasp, I think… well, no, it’s the whole idea of being stung if it looks like one. Not all flying insects, no… I mean, a fly buzzing around is annoying, and mosquitoes—well, everyone hates those, don’t they? My sister used to keep citronella candles all over her patio, and they never worked, not really.
